Evidence: `packages/domain/src/money.ts:35-44 divRoundHalfUp on BigInt; :87-100 scaleDecimal parses decimal strings digit-wise; :139-149 pctOfPaise/bpOfPaise in basis points`, `packages/domain/src/dues.ts:171-196 installmentAmounts: amount_i = round(agreed×cum_i) − round(agreed×cum_{i−1}) so the sum is exactly the agreed amount`, `packages/domain/test/dues.test.ts:60-90 — ₹1 Cr 60/40 table, 33.33/33.33/33.34 on odd paise, property test over random splits (124 domain tests green in my run)`, `packages/contracts/src/common.ts:16-27 PaiseSchema = z.int().min(0).max(2^53−1); :38-43 PercentSchema ≤ 2 decimals; packages/contracts/src/modules/rateCards.ts:34-46 PLC paise values refined to integers`, `packages/db/src/schema/_common.ts:40-44 bigint paise columns; packages/db/src/client.ts:118 bigint parsed to JS number`, `apps/api/src/modules/money/services/ledger.ts:120-128 demandAmountsFor reuses the same rounding; bookings.ts:198-210 agreement revision recomputes every demand telescopically; apps/api/test/integration/money.test.ts worked example 'ten rows to the rupee' (32 tests green in my run)`

### B5. Canonical forms / normalisation — 830/1000 (v1: 590)

**Strengths.** Canonicalisation happens at the schema edge (phone, PAN, Aadhaar, IFSC, email, codes) and again in the service layer, identifiers are encrypted with keyed hashes for dedupe, duplicate checks are serialised with advisory locks, and the materialised copies that matter for money (booking summary, unit counts, portal phone) are maintained inside the same transaction and reconciled nightly.

**Gaps.** Phones are stored as a 10-digit national form rather than E.164, which is fine for the spec but blocks NRI buyers and international formatting later. flats.flat_type_code (inventory.ts:270) and payments.mode_code are denormalised copies with no sync when a master's code is edited (flatTypesKind has no afterWrite, kinds.ts:181-250), so lists and CSVs can show a stale code. Dedupe relies on the lock-plus-SELECT pattern because there is no unique index on (company_id, phone) or (company_id, pan_hash). co_applicants carry pan_hash but no aadhaar_hash (customers.ts:79-83), so co-applicant Aadhaar duplicates are undetectable, and customers.email is plain text rather than citext (customers.ts:28).

**Second auditor.** Verified: PhoneSchema strips +91/0 to 10 digits, PAN/IFSC/GSTIN upperTrim, Aadhaar stripSpaces, email lowerTrim (contracts/common.ts:64-115); service layer re-canonicalises and encrypts PAN/Aadhaar with keyed hashes + last4 (customerService.ts:44-63); dedupe is advisory-lock + SELECT with 409 DUPLICATE_CUSTOMER (customerService.ts:203-227); portal phone is kept in step on customer phone change (customerService.ts:348-352, and the crm-adversarial test for it passed in my run). Confirmed gaps: no unique index on (company_id, phone)/(company_id, pan_hash) so a seed/raw path can duplicate; co_applicants has pan_hash but no aadhaar_hash (customers.ts:79-83); customers.email is text while users.email is citext (identity.ts:69); flats.flat_type_code is a denormalised copy and the flatTypes master kind has no afterWrite (the only afterWrite in kinds.ts is the payment-plan one at :373). Additional inconsistency the first auditor missed: the two erasure endpoints write different 'canonical' placeholder phones — admin/erase.ts:31-35 uses '0' + 9 id-derived digits while crm/customers.ts:350 uses '0000' + last 6 digits of the customer code — so erased identities are not even normalised consistently within the system. 830.

**World-best would.** Store E.164 with a separate national display form, enforce dedupe with partial unique indexes on the keyed hashes, keep every denormalised code in sync via a trigger or an afterWrite hook on the master, and hash every encrypted identifier (co-applicant Aadhaar included) so dedupe is uniform.

Evidence: `packages/contracts/src/common.ts:64-78 PhoneSchema (strip +91/0/punctuation → 10-digit), :84-86 PANSchema upperTrim, :93 AadhaarSchema, :102 EmailSchema lower, :126 CodeSchema upper`, `packages/domain/src/canonical.ts:6-25 normalizeIndianPhone; :131-135 isValidAadhaar (Verhoeff); :26-45 PAN normalise/mask`, `apps/api/src/modules/crm/customerService.ts:44-63 canonicalPhone / panColumns / aadhaarColumns → AES-GCM ciphertext + HMAC keyed hash + last4; :203-227 advisory locks + 409 DUPLICATE_CUSTOMER on phone or PAN hash`, `apps/api/src/lib/crypto.ts:37-39 keyedHash (HMAC-SHA256 with a derived key)`, `apps/api/src/modules/inventory/service.ts:150-162 refreshUnitCounts in one statement; money/services/ledger.ts:72-85 writeSummary transactional; integrity.ts nightly reconciliation; customerService.ts:348-352 portal login phone kept in step with customer phone`, `packages/db/src/schema/customers.ts:62-64 indexes on (company_id, phone) and (company_id, pan_hash)`

### B6. Snapshotting & versioning of structural data — 840/1000 (v1: 600)

**Strengths.** The three snapshot levels the spec asks for exist and are guarded: flats freeze rooms/areas/price at generation, towers freeze their stage sequence as rows, bookings freeze flat, rate and plan (with stage names), and every change to a booking's agreement or plan is an append-only revision row plus a ledger event. Master edits therefore never silently alter existing records, and the guards (all Available / all PENDING) are enforced in transactions.

**Gaps.** Consistency after master edits is one-directional: editing a flat type's rooms/areas/code, renaming a stage, or replacing a payment plan's installments (kinds.ts:373-380 deletes and re-inserts them) leaves existing snapshots silently stale with no 'differs from master' indicator and no bulk re-snapshot for still-Available flats, so the grid can show old areas next to a changed master. Payment plan masters have no version history of their own — the only record of what a plan looked like before an edit is the audit before/after JSON. Towers under construction keep the stage name they were created with forever. The booking flat snapshot omits specification items (portal/common.ts:49 reads flatTypes.specItemIds live), so a spec change does leak into a sold flat's portal view.

**Second auditor.** Verified the three snapshot levels and their guards: flats carry rooms/areas/price snapshots and can only be regenerated or re-typed while Available (inventory/blocks.ts:229-257, flats.ts:237-253); block stage sequences re-snapshot only while every stage is PENDING (blocks.ts:248-250, 268-281; service.ts:226-230); bookings freeze flat_snapshot and plan_snapshot (money/services/bookings.ts:107-121) and every agreement or plan change is an append-only revision row plus rebuild (bookings.ts:179-205, 212-245; schema bookings.ts:159-200). The gaps hold: grep finds no 'stale' or 'differs from master' handling anywhere in inventory/masters; the payment-plan master replaces its installments by DELETE + INSERT on every edit with no plan version row (kinds.ts:373-382), and nothing stops editing a plan that assignments or bookings already use (usage is checked only on deactivate, factory.ts:172), so the only history of what a plan looked like is the audit JSON; the portal reads flatTypes.specItemIds live (portal/common.ts:51) so spec changes leak into sold flats. Master edits never corrupt existing records, which is the SPEC requirement, but 'consistency after master edits' is unaddressed rather than solved; 840.

**World-best would.** Version master rows (plan_versions, flat_type_versions) and point snapshots at a version id, surface a per-row 'snapshot stale vs master' flag with an explicit, audited 'apply master to Available flats' action, and include every buyer-visible attribute (spec items) in the booking snapshot.

Evidence: `apps/api/src/modules/inventory/service.ts:338 — flats generated with rooms_snapshot/areas_snapshot/areas and price breakup from the flat type; :210-222 snapshotBlockStages, :226-230 allStagesPending`, `apps/api/src/modules/inventory/blocks.ts:222-227,248-257 — structural edits and regeneration only while every flat is Available; stage sequence replaced only while every stage is PENDING`, `apps/api/src/modules/inventory/flats.ts:237-253 — flat-type change re-snapshots rooms/areas (+ optional reprice) only while Available`, `apps/api/src/modules/money/services/bookings.ts:107-121 — booking stores flat_snapshot (rooms, areas, price, breakup) and plan_snapshot; plans.ts:72 snapshot carries trigger_stage_name`, `apps/api/src/modules/money/services/bookings.ts:171-205 agreement revisions with before/after rows and telescoped demand recomputation; :212-245 plan change writes booking_plan_revisions, re-snapshots, rebuilds and re-raises dues`, `packages/db/src/schema/bookings.ts:159-200 agreement_revisions / booking_plan_revisions append-only; inventory.ts:216-234 block_stages snapshot rows`

### B7. Migrations & schema evolution — 720/1000 (v1: 450)

**Strengths.** Schema evolution is a committed, journaled SQL chain with a second idempotent layer for triggers, tested from an empty database and for re-runs, wired into CI and into the API boot. The one post-launch change so far (0001) shows the process works end to end, and the greenfield Postgres design avoids the milestone_*/installment_* dual-field debt of the previous system entirely.

**Gaps.** There are no down migrations and no expand/contract policy, so a bad deploy has no rehearsed rollback. Boot-time migrations take no advisory lock (server.ts:17-20), so `docker compose --scale api=N` on first deploy races N migrators. CI never runs `drizzle-kit check`, so drift between the TypeScript schema and the committed SQL is only caught for column names, not types, defaults or indexes. The legacy concern is absent rather than solved: there is no importer or dual-read path from the v1 Mongo data (only the demo seed), so moving a live builder onto v2 has no tooling. EXPECTED_TABLE_COUNT = 77 is hard-coded in two tests and will be hand-bumped.

**Second auditor.** Verified the good parts: committed drizzle journal + 0001_central_fixes.sql, idempotent CREATE OR REPLACE / IF NOT EXISTS trigger layer (0001_triggers.sql), migrate.ts resolves source/bundle/Docker layouts, migrate.test.ts covers fresh-DB and re-run, CI runs migrate before tests (ci.yml:33); 50 db tests green in my run. But the first auditor understated the boot race: it is not a hypothetical `--scale api=N` — the shipped docker-compose.yml:40 declares `deploy: { replicas: 2 }` for the api service with RUN_MIGRATIONS_ON_BOOT='true' and SEED_ON_BOOT defaulting to true (config.ts:28-29), and API_WORKERS defaults to 1 so each container runs migrate() then seed() itself (server.ts:93-102). I read drizzle-orm's pg-core dialect.migrate(): it runs CREATE SCHEMA/TABLE IF NOT EXISTS and the pending statements in a plain transaction with no advisory lock, so two containers on an empty database race (the loser crashes on 'already exists' and restarts). Worse, ensureBootstrap (seed/bootstrap.ts:27-35) does SELECT companies LIMIT 1 then INSERT with no lock and no unique constraint on companies, and it spends ~a second hashing six argon2 passwords inside that window, so two replicas that both find the schema already migrated (e.g. after the documented `pnpm db:migrate` quickstart in DEPLOY.md:7) can each create a company and seed a demo — permanently corrupting the single-tenant assumption that login and the public site rely on. No `drizzle-kit check` in CI (packages/db/package.json:11 only has generate), no down migrations, no v1 import tooling. 720.

**World-best would.** Add a migration advisory lock, a `drizzle-kit check` gate in CI, reversible migrations with an expand/contract convention, and a tested v1→v2 import tool (idempotent, with field-mapping for milestone_* → installment_*) so an existing builder's data can be cut over with a dry-run report.

Evidence: `packages/db/drizzle/0000_initial.sql (77 CREATE TABLE), 0001_central_fixes.sql, meta/_journal.json + snapshots — drizzle-kit generated SQL committed`, `packages/db/src/migrate.ts:48-56 — citext extension, drizzle journal, then idempotent src/sql/*.sql; :22-40 folder resolution for source, bundle and Docker layouts`, `packages/db/test/migrate.test.ts:38-66 fresh database migrate/verify/drop, :69-78 idempotent re-run, :80-99 journal and SQL consistency; data-model.test.ts:39 Drizzle↔DB column drift check (50 db tests green in my run)`, `packages/db/src/sql/0001_triggers.sql:1-2,65-85 — hand-written SQL is CREATE OR REPLACE / IF NOT EXISTS so every deploy can re-apply it`, `apps/api/src/server.ts:17-20 RUN_MIGRATIONS_ON_BOOT; .github/workflows/ci.yml:33 'pnpm --filter @kkt/db migrate && … test'`, `docs/TODO-integration.md items 3, 4, 11 — documents.version, new indexes and category_id NOT NULL delivered as migration 0001`

### B8. Soft delete / audit immutability — 830/1000 (v1: 530)

**Strengths.** The audit log is immutable at the database level including TRUNCATE, payments and demands cannot be deleted by any code path (reversal/void with mandatory reason instead), masters and documents are soft-deleted, and the ledger rebuild is a deterministic re-derivation from guarded source rows under the booking lock, with a dry-run (reconcile) and tests that manufacture drift and prove the rebuild fixes it.

**Gaps.** ledger_entries carry no delete guard at all, so any bug or raw SQL can wipe a ledger and the only recovery is a rebuild from source rows; the rebuild itself is a destructive delete-and-reinsert (ledger.ts:240-242) with no retained prior version or correction event, which is weaker than an append-only correction model. TRUNCATE bypasses the row-level no-delete triggers on payments and demands (seed/run.ts:117 relies on this; only audit_logs has a TRUNCATE trigger). Flats (blocks.ts:255,298), CMS content (cms.ts:266), stage photo links (construction/service.ts:601), co_applicants (customerService.ts:305) and plan installments (kinds.ts:377) are hard-deleted — audited, but with history lost. Audit rows legitimately contain PII in before/after and there is no field-level redaction design for erasure.

**Second auditor.** Verified: audit_logs raises on UPDATE/DELETE (row) and TRUNCATE (statement); payments and demands raise on DELETE (0001_triggers.sql:7-44); audit.test.ts covers Drizzle and raw SQL, rollback-on-tamper, and void/reverse instead of delete (7 tests green in my run); stripSensitive removes hashes/ciphertext/tokens from before/after (lib/audit.ts:26-43); rebuild() is lockBooking → derive from source rows → DELETE ledger rows → INSERT → writeSummary (ledger.ts:216-243). My grep of .delete( across apps/api/src matches the first auditor's list (flats, ledger_entries, CMS rows, stage photos, co_applicants, plan installments, drafts, scopes, presets, amenities, rate card lines). Two things lower the score. First, the no-delete triggers are a weaker last line of defence than they look: the API connects as the Postgres superuser in the shipped compose (DATABASE_URL postgres:…@postgres:5432), and the reseed path demonstrates the bypass in-app with `set local session_replication_role = replica` before TRUNCATE … CASCADE (seed/run.ts:116-117) — any code running as that role can disable every trigger, including audit immutability. Second, the CRM erase route hard-deletes co_applicants (crm/customers.ts:357) where the admin route anonymises them, so history handling is inconsistent even for the same business action. 830.

**World-best would.** Guard ledger_entries (and TRUNCATE on payments/demands) with triggers that only a dedicated rebuild role can bypass, implement recompute as an append-only 'rebuild epoch' that supersedes rather than deletes rows, soft-delete structural rows (flats, CMS, photos) everywhere, and store audit PII in an encrypted side column so erasure can shred the key without touching the immutable log.

Evidence: `packages/db/src/sql/0001_triggers.sql:7-22 — audit_logs raises on UPDATE/DELETE (row) and TRUNCATE (statement); :27-44 payments_no_delete and demands_no_delete triggers; :24-26 states ledger_entries are deliberately unguarded for recompute`, `packages/db/test/audit.test.ts — 7 tests: Drizzle and raw-SQL UPDATE/DELETE/TRUNCATE rejected, tamper inside a transaction rolls everything back, payments/demands DELETE rejected (green in my run)`, `apps/api/src/lib/audit.ts:26-43 stripSensitive removes hashes/ciphertext/tokens from before/after before insert`, `apps/api/src/modules/money/services/ledger.ts:216-243 rebuild(write): lockBooking → derive from demands/payments/charges/revisions → DELETE ledger rows → INSERT derived rows → writeSummary; apps/api/test/integration/admin.test.ts:405-424 manufactured drift detected by reconcile and fixed by recompute (passed in my run)`, `apps/api/src/modules/masters/factory.ts:172-178 soft delete with usage block; documents/routes.ts:173 is_deleted soft delete; money.test 'reversal … receipt cancelled (never deleted)'`, `grep of tx.delete( across apps/api/src: hard deletes exist only on flats (inventory/blocks.ts:255,298), ledger_entries (ledger.ts:240), CMS rows (web/cms.ts:266), stage_photos (construction/service.ts:601), co_applicants, plan installments, drafts, scopes, presets`

### B9. Backup, restore, right-to-erasure — 660/1000 (v1: 350)

**Strengths.** The spec's two owner-facing requirements are implemented well: a Super-Admin backup export that is tenant-scoped, strips secrets by default, is zipped with a manifest and restore notes, and is handed out through forgery-tested signed links; and a right-to-erasure that refuses while a booking is active, anonymises every operational copy of the PII (not just the customer row), revokes portal sessions, removes document bytes, keeps the financial rows and writes an audit trail — all covered by integration tests.

**Gaps.** There is no infrastructure backup at all in the shipped deployment: no pg_dump/pgBackRest/WAL-archiving service in docker-compose.yml or infra/, no retention policy, no point-in-time recovery, and the application export is capped at 200k rows per table so it is not a complete backup for a large builder. RESTORE.md is a manual psql \copy procedure and no restore drill is automated or tested. Erasure leaves PII behind in immutable audit_logs before/after snapshots, login_events.identifier (the portal phone), notification titles/bodies and service_request_messages.author_name, and storage deletion has no retry queue. There is no customer-initiated data export or erasure request flow (DPDP data-principal rights), only a Super Admin action.

**Second auditor.** Verified the API side: tenant-scoped per-table JSON export with sensitive-column stripping, 200k-row cap, manifest and signed links (admin/backup.ts:1-60,136-154,240-260), and a thorough admin erasure that refuses with an active booking, anonymises customer/co-applicants/portal login/documents/leads/site visits/enquiries/testimonials, revokes sessions and keeps financial rows (admin/erase.ts:62-150); admin-adversarial (11 tests) and crm-adversarial (16 tests, incl. erasure) passed in my run. Three findings the first auditor missed cut the score. (1) Neither feature is reachable by the owner: grep of apps/erp/src finds no call to /admin/backup or any erase endpoint and no admin page for them (features/admin contains Users/Roles/Settings/Sessions/AuditLog/Notifications only), so SPEC §2 'Super Admin can download a full backup export' and 'right to erasure' exist only as curl-able routes despite the 543-page ERP QA. (2) There are two divergent erasure implementations: POST /customers/:id/erase (crm/customers.ts:338-370) skips site visits, enquiries, testimonials, document soft-delete and storage removal, hard-deletes co-applicants, uses a different placeholder phone and is not idempotent, while POST /admin/customers/:id/erase does all of it — a caller using the CRM route believes PII is gone when copies remain. (3) Confirmed no infrastructure backup: docker-compose.yml has only a pgdata volume, infra/postgres/postgresql.conf has no archive_mode/WAL archiving, DEPLOY.md:54 merely says 'also schedule pg_dump'; the app export is built fully in memory (ZipWriter) so it is not a complete backup for a large builder, and restore is an untested manual \copy procedure. PII also persists in immutable audit before/after and login_events.identifier after erasure. 660.

**World-best would.** Run continuous WAL archiving with nightly base backups to object storage, a scheduled restore-and-verify job (restore into a scratch database, run the integrity report, alert on failure), and document RPO/RTO; design erasure with an encrypted PII side-channel for audit/login rows so a key shred completes the erasure, plus a self-service portal export/erasure request with a tracked SLA.

Evidence: `apps/api/src/modules/admin/backup.ts:1-8,31,36-42,136-154,240 — tenant-scoped per-table JSON export with manifest.json, RESTORE.md, sensitive-column stripping, 24 h retention, 15-min signed link, 200,000-row cap per table, buildBackup exported for cron use`, `apps/api/src/modules/admin/erase.ts:62-150 — eraseCustomer: 409 HAS_ACTIVE_BOOKING, anonymises customer, co-applicants, portal login (sessions revoked), documents (soft-deleted + bytes removed), leads, site visits, enquiries, testimonials; financial rows counted and kept; audit row`, `apps/api/test/integration/admin.test.ts:116-247 backup (ZIP validity, stripping, signed-link forgery) and :249-330 erasure tests; admin-adversarial.test.ts:106-160 tenant isolation of exports — 14/15 passed in my run (the reseed case needs every module loaded)`, `docs/DEPLOY.md 'Backups' — app export plus 'also schedule pg_dump at the infrastructure level'; docker-compose.yml:75 only a pgdata volume, no backup service; infra/postgres/postgresql.conf has no archive_mode/WAL archiving`, `apps/api/src/modules/admin/erase.ts:139-146 — storage removal is best-effort after commit (warn on failure, no retry)`, `apps/api/src/modules/crm/customerService.ts:330,356 and apps/api/src/modules/auth/service.ts:172-174 — customer name/phone persist in immutable audit before/after and login_events.identifier after erasure`

### B10. Multi-tenancy readiness — 720/1000 (v1: 370)

**Strengths.** The data layer is genuinely tenant-ready as the spec asks: company_id NOT NULL on every business table is enforced by a test, every business-key uniqueness and every numbering sequence is company-scoped, the staff/portal contexts carry companyId and the central loaders (masters, bookings, documents, portal ownership, reports, dashboards cache, backup export) filter by it, and there are cross-tenant tests for documents and backups.

**Gaps.** Flipping the switch would break authentication first: staff login resolves a user by email alone (staff.ts:86) and portal login by phone alone (portal.ts:53), while uniqueness is per company, so two tenants sharing an email or phone would log into an arbitrary row. The public website is bound to the first-created company (shared.ts:122-125) with a company-less cache key, and website slugs are global. There is no Postgres row-level security or tenant GUC as a second line of defence, and roughly a hundred internal lookups by bare id (e.g. crm/leadService.ts:156,177,326; money/services/dues.ts:176) rely on the caller having scoped first. login_events.company_id is nullable (identity.ts:150). Only backups and documents have adversarial two-company tests; no systematic cross-tenant suite covers the other endpoints.

**Second auditor.** Verified the foundation: data-model.test.ts:425 asserts company_id uuid NOT NULL on every business table (green in my run), business keys and sequences are company-scoped (identity.ts:177-186; customers.ts:109), staff context takes companyId from the users row (plugins/auth.ts:80-135), and loaders filter by company (portal/common.ts:66-76, backup tableScope). Cross-tenant tests exist only in documents.test.ts and admin-adversarial.test.ts (grep confirmed). The blocking defects are real and I found they are reachable today, not only after 'flipping the switch': staff login is `where(eq(users.email, email))` (auth/staff.ts:86) and portal login `where(eq(portalUsers.phone, phone))` (auth/portal.ts:53) with uniqueness per company; the public site is bound to the first-created company (web/shared.ts:122-125) with company-less cache keys; website slugs are globally unique (inventory.ts:153). Because the default compose runs two API replicas that each run ensureBootstrap without a lock (see Migrations), a second company row can appear in a single-tenant deployment by accident, at which point email-only login resolves to an arbitrary tenant's user with the same seeded credentials. No RLS/tenant GUC; login_events.company_id nullable; ~100 bare-id lookups (e.g. leadService.ts:156,177,326; dues.ts:176) depend on callers scoping first. SPEC §1 only asks for 'company id on every record so multi-tenant can be switched on later', which is met, but the switch would break authentication first. 720.

**World-best would.** Add a tenant resolver (host/subdomain or company code at login) so every credential lookup is (company_id, identifier), enable Postgres RLS with a per-transaction tenant GUC set by the db plugin, scope public caches and slugs per company, and add a generated two-tenant conformance test that hits every registered route with a foreign company's ids expecting 404.

Evidence: `packages/db/test/data-model.test.ts:126 — test: every business table carries company_id uuid NOT NULL referencing companies; invariants.test.ts:72-85 — same code allowed in another company, duplicate in the same company rejected`, `packages/db/src/schema/identity.ts:177-186 sequences PK(company_id, scope, fy); users UNIQUE(company_id, email); customers.ts:109 portal_users UNIQUE(company_id, phone)`, `apps/api/src/plugins/auth.ts:80,135 companyId taken from the users row; masters/factory.ts:24-30, money/common.ts:62-68, portal/common.ts:66-76, documents/routes.ts:100 — loaders filter by company_id; reports/services/dashboards.ts:47-50 cache key includes companyId`, `apps/api/src/modules/admin/backup.ts:136-154 tenant-scoped export; apps/api/test/integration/admin-adversarial.test.ts:132 and documents.test.ts:632 cross-company isolation tests`, `apps/api/src/modules/auth/staff.ts:86 where(eq(users.email, email)) and auth/portal.ts:53 where(eq(portalUsers.phone, phone)) — login lookups are not company-qualified`, `apps/api/src/modules/web/shared.ts:122-125 publicCompanyId() = first company by created_at; shared.ts:103 public cache key carries no company; inventory.ts:160 project_website_settings.slug globally unique`

## Group C — Performance & Scale — **6990 / 10,000** (previous implementation: 3860)

| # | Factor | Previous (v1) | First score | Final score |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Measured latency vs targets | 250 | 700 | **670** |
| 2 | Throughput & concurrency model | 390 | 760 | **720** |
| 3 | Caching strategy | 340 | 740 | **725** |
| 4 | Horizontal scalability / statelessness | 380 | 740 | **700** |
| 5 | Hot-path efficiency | 360 | 780 | **740** |
| 6 | Pagination & large-list handling | 430 | 760 | **740** |
| 7 | Connection pooling & DB load | 430 | 720 | **700** |
| 8 | Readiness for 5 lakh | 210 | 630 | **590** |
| 9 | Frontend performance | 570 | 720 | **715** |
| 10 | Load-testing rigor & performance observability | 500 | 700 | **690** |

### C1. Measured latency vs targets — 670/1000 (v1: 250)

**Strengths.** Real measured numbers exist for every hot endpoint, before and after tuning, and at 200 concurrent hammering connections all list/detail endpoints except the bookings page meet p95 < 300 ms on a shared 4-vCPU box; the doc is candid about what misses and why, and server time per request (≈ 4 ms) is far inside the spec's 'server time' wording.

**Gaps.** At 1,000 zero-think-time connections every list endpoint misses (p95 0.5–1.7 s) and the bookings page shows a 16.5 s p99 with timeouts, i.e. no latency bound by default (API_MAX_INFLIGHT is off). The bookings list, the ERP's most used screen, misses even at 200 connections because each row carries full plan/flat snapshots (65 KB per 25 rows). The ERP '< 1.5 s page' target was never measured (docs/qa/erp.md has no timings) and the website numbers are warm-server networkidle times on localhost, not 4G/Lighthouse figures. All measurements are on a toy seed (130 flats, 10 bookings), so latency at realistic row counts is unknown.

**Second auditor.** The first auditor's numbers are accurately transcribed from docs/PERFORMANCE.md and the harness is real (apps/api/loadtest/run.ts), so the 200-connection result (every list except the 25-row bookings page under 300 ms p95) stands. Two things make 700 slightly generous. First, the dataset is 10 bookings / 130 flats / 12 customers (docs/SEED.md), and the default sorts of the three biggest ERP lists have no index that supports them: GET /bookings defaults to -booking_date filtered only by company, but the only date index is (company_id, project_id, status, booking_date) (0000_initial.sql:1579), and GET /customers defaults to -created_at with no (company_id, created_at) index at all (customers.ts:147; indexes at 0000_initial.sql:1589-1591). On a lakh-row table every page is a sort of the whole company's rows plus five joins, so the measured 4 ms server time is a property of the seed, not of the query shape. Second, the website figure under load is not measurable with this harness because the Next.js server's own public-API calls share one rate-limit bucket (see finding 1); the 556-748 ms QA timings were taken at single-user traffic. The ERP < 1.5 s target is indeed unmeasured (docs/qa/erp.md has no timings). Per-request server time of the hot paths is genuinely small and the doc is honest, which keeps this well above 600.

**World-best would.** Measure on a dedicated API node with the database elsewhere and a production-sized dataset, publish p50/p95/p99 per endpoint under a realistic think-time model, enforce a latency SLO in CI, and trim the bookings list payload (snapshots on demand) so every list page clears 300 ms with margin at 1,000 concurrent users.

Evidence: `docs/PERFORMANCE.md 'Run 2026-10-25T20:42:04' (200 conns, API_WORKERS=auto): /projects p95 102 ms, /flats 209, /grid 137, /customers 111, /bookings/:id 142, /ledger 146, /dashboard 34, /public/projects 15, /search 50 — only GET /bookings (25 rows, 65 KB) misses at p95 404 ms`, `docs/PERFORMANCE.md 'Run 2026-10-25T20:49:48' (1,000 conns, zero think time): /projects p95 493, /flats 832, /bookings 1,731 ms with p99 16.5 s and 60 timeouts; auth/dashboard/public/search stay < 300 ms`, `docs/PERFORMANCE.md 'Honest assessment': per-request server time of the bookings page ≈ 4 ms; the 1,000-connection miss is attributed to CPU on a shared 4-vCPU box (Postgres, Redis and autocannon co-located)`, `apps/api/loadtest/run.ts:188-211 — 14 scenarios incl. a POST /payments write path; run.ts:150-163 settle() drains the backlog between scenarios so rows do not contaminate each other`, `docs/qa/web.md:13-35 — every website page settles 556–748 ms at 1440 and 390 px (< 2 s target); docs/qa/erp.md contains no page-timing numbers, so the '< 1.5 s ERP pages' target (docs/SPEC.md:255) is unmeasured`, `docs/SPEC.md:46 — target: 1,000 concurrent staff, list pages < 300 ms server time`

### C2. Throughput & concurrency model — 720/1000 (v1: 390)

**Strengths.** Fastify + compiled Zod serializers + prepared statements + cluster workers give a measured 5–6k rps per 4 vCPU on authenticated reads and 20k rps on cached public reads; per-request round trips are tight (1 Redis + 2–3 SQL for a list) and the search path cancels statements server-side instead of leaking them.

**Gaps.** CPU-heavy work still runs in the API process: pdfkit report/receipt rendering and sharp renditions execute inside request handlers, and the in-process job worker runs in HTTP worker 0 by default, so nightly integrity or a 10k-row PDF competes with staff requests. Pool waits are unbounded, so under overload requests queue on the database pool (p99 16 s) unless the opt-in API_MAX_INFLIGHT knob is set. The production compose file does not set API_WORKERS, so the measured multi-process throughput is not what `docker compose up` deploys. Brotli/gzip of every > 1 KB response (app.ts:72) is a non-trivial share of the per-request CPU on 65 KB pages.

**Second auditor.** Every cited mechanism checks out: node:cluster supervisor with restart (server.ts:62-91), one Redis MGET per authenticated request (auth.ts:70-88), one page + one count + one JSON-aggregating related statement per list (list.ts:190, views.ts:179-201), compiled serializers and prepared statements. But the gap between the measured configuration and what ships is larger than a minor deduction: apps/api/Dockerfile sets only NODE_ENV and docker-compose.yml sets no API_WORKERS, so `docker compose up` runs the single-process 'after tuning, API_WORKERS=1' column (bookings p95 911 ms at 200 conns), not the headline numbers; RUN_JOBS_IN_API defaults true (config.ts:30) so the api container also consumes the BullMQ queue alongside the dedicated worker container; sharp renditions (documents/service.ts:128-139, 188-196) and pdfkit (reports/routes/reports.ts:60-66, services/pdf.ts:11) run inside request handlers despite ARCHITECTURE §6 stating heavy work never does; and postPayment is ~15 serial statements including row-by-row demand updates (dues.ts:194-206). Compression is brotli quality 4 (@fastify/compress default), so that cost is modest, and ETags are computed before compression (instance onSend runs before the route-level compress hook), so that path is correct. Solid engineering, but the deployed default is one process with mixed roles, which is what the score must reflect.

**World-best would.** Move PDF/image rendering and all scheduled jobs to the separate worker container by default, bound pool acquisition and in-flight requests out of the box, ship the compose/K8s manifests with API_WORKERS=auto and CPU limits, and offload compression to the edge so the API process only does JSON.

Evidence: `apps/api/src/server.ts:12-14,62-91 — node:cluster supervisor forks API_WORKERS (auto = one per CPU) stateless HTTP workers, restarts crashed ones, forwards signals; docs/PERFORMANCE.md: 1 → 4 workers lifted /auth/me from 2,571 to 5,319 rps and /projects from 1,022 to 2,703 rps at 200 conns`, `apps/api/src/plugins/auth.ts:70-88 — one Redis MGET per authenticated request (perm version + cached user + revocation), DB only on a 30 s cache miss; apps/api/src/lib/list.ts:190 — a list page is one page query + one count query in parallel, plus one JSON-aggregating related query (views.ts:185-201)`, `apps/api/src/plugins/loadShed.ts:11-28 — per-process in-flight cap answering 503 + Retry-After: 2 (off by default, config.ts:50); apps/api/src/app.ts:74-82 under-pressure with maxEventLoopDelay 1000 ms`, `apps/api/src/modules/documents/service.ts:128-139,188-196 — sharp thumb/medium renditions are generated inside the upload request; apps/api/src/modules/reports/routes/reports.ts:60-66 — report PDFs are rendered with pdfkit inside the request handler (ARCHITECTURE §6 claims heavy work never runs in a request)`, `apps/api/src/config.ts:30 + server.ts:99 — RUN_JOBS_IN_API defaults true, so the BullMQ worker (daily dues, nightly integrity) shares HTTP worker 0's event loop; docker-compose.yml api service sets no API_WORKERS, so each production container runs a single process despite DEPLOY.md telling operators to use auto`, `docs/PERFORMANCE.md 'What profiling found': ≈ 0.7 ms CPU per simple authenticated request (16 % socket writes, 19 % Drizzle, 8 % GC) → ~1.5k rps per core`

### C3. Caching strategy — 725/1000 (v1: 340)

**Strengths.** Every server-side cache lives in Redis with correct cross-worker invalidation (generation counters for public and permission caches, explicit busts for company settings), public responses carry CDN-ready headers, media and ERP assets are immutable for a year, and the dashboard cache turns the heaviest read into a 34 ms p95.

**Gaps.** ETags are computed after the handler on no-store responses, so conditional GETs save nothing server-side and no client sends If-None-Match; the documented 'ERP uses ETag/If-None-Match' (ARCHITECTURE §6) is not true on the client. `cached()` has no stampede protection — when a hot public key expires at 20k rps every worker rebuilds it simultaneously. The dashboard cache is per user, so with hundreds of staff the hit ratio collapses. The website is force-dynamic with detail fetches no-store, so every project page is rendered and fetched per request (only the API's own 60 s Redis cache stands between visitors and Postgres). Caches, rate limits, revocation markers and queue state share one LRU Redis with no persistence, so memory pressure can evict security-relevant keys.

**Second auditor.** Re-read confirms the strong parts: the public cache key is generation-prefixed (web/shared.ts:26,103,117-119) so invalidation is race-free across workers, cache keys are built from validated inputs (shared.ts:88-96), not-found is cached (shared.ts:104-108), the permission cache is versioned in Redis (auth.ts:70-88, cache.ts:28-34), the company cache is Redis-backed with explicit invalidation (lib/company.ts:13-26), and dashboards are cached 15 s. Also confirmed: cached() is a plain get-or-compute with no single-flight or soft TTL (cache.ts:4-14); the dashboard key includes user id (dashboards.ts:47-50) so hit ratio scales inversely with staff count; apps/erp/src never sends If-None-Match (grep: no hits), so the ARCHITECTURE §6 sentence 'ERP uses ETag/If-None-Match on lists' is false, as is 'website pages via ISR' (apps/web/app/layout.tsx:17 force-dynamic; prerender-manifest lists only /icon.svg). One Redis with allkeys-lru and no persistence holds revocation markers and BullMQ state alongside caches (docker-compose.yml redis command). Slightly lower than the first auditor because two documented cache tiers do not exist and the website's SSR fetches carry no visitor identity, which turns the public rate limiter into a cache-independent ceiling (finding 1).

**World-best would.** Serve conditional requests from a per-list version source (max(updated_at)/row count) before the handler, add single-flight/soft-TTL refresh to the read-through cache, key dashboards by role+scope with a short per-user overlay, make public website pages ISR/CDN-cacheable with tag-based revalidation from CMS writes, and separate the cache Redis from the durable-state Redis.

Evidence: `apps/api/src/modules/web/shared.ts:16-26,102-119 — public GETs cached in Redis 60 s under a generation counter (pub:version), bumped on every CMS/visit write so stale entries are never read again (no SCAN race); Cache-Control: public, max-age=60, s-maxage=60, stale-while-revalidate=300 for CDNs`, `apps/api/src/lib/cache.ts:4-14,29-34 — read-through cached() with best-effort Redis; permission cache (auth.ts:71-87) keyed by a Redis version bumped from 20+ write sites (users, roles, portalAccess, erase) → multi-instance safe; apps/api/src/lib/company.ts:13-26 30 s company cache with explicit invalidate`, `apps/api/src/modules/reports/services/dashboards.ts:21,47-57 — dashboard cached 15 s per role + user id + scope + project (measured 9,212 rps at 200 conns)`, `apps/api/src/app.ts:73 + plugins/requestContext.ts:13 — @fastify/etag on every response but staff responses default to Cache-Control: no-store; docs/PERFORMANCE.md: the 304 scenario gives 746 vs 657 rps, i.e. the ETag is hashed after the handler ran and saves bandwidth, not server time; apps/erp/src/lib/api.ts never sends If-None-Match (grep)`, `apps/erp/src/app.tsx:20-27 — TanStack Query staleTime 15 s / gcTime 5 min; apps/web/lib/api.ts:151-158,183 — Next Data Cache 60 s with tags for lists, detail resources deliberately no-store; apps/web/app/layout.tsx:17 — export const dynamic = "force-dynamic" on the root layout, so no page HTML is ever statically cached`, `docker-compose.yml redis: --maxmemory 512mb --maxmemory-policy allkeys-lru --save "" — one LRU Redis shared by caches, rate limits, idempotency fast path, revocation markers and BullMQ`

### C4. Horizontal scalability / statelessness — 700/1000 (v1: 380)

**Strengths.** Nothing that matters for correctness lives in process memory: auth, permissions, rate limits, idempotency, public cache generation and job scheduling are all Redis/Postgres-backed, the cluster workers are interchangeable, Caddy load-balances with least_conn, and a read-replica handle is already threaded through every read path.

**Gaps.** Two things stop a real multi-node deployment today: the object-storage adapter is a stub that throws for S3, so uploads and media are tied to a local volume; and Prometheus metrics are per worker process behind a shared port, so observability degrades the moment API_WORKERS > 1. The architecture doc oversells readiness — partition scripts and a PgBouncer-fronted prod compose are described but absent. Each API container also runs an in-process job worker in HTTP worker 0, mixing roles that should scale independently.

**Second auditor.** Request-path state is correctly externalised (JWT + Redis revocation, Redis rate limits in a MULTI, idempotency with a Redis NX lock and a durable Postgres row, per-process pg_class estimate cache that only picks a counting strategy). The deductions the first auditor made are all verified: storage.ts:34 throws for STORAGE_DRIVER=s3, metrics.ts is per-process Maps behind a shared cluster port, packages/db/partitions does not exist, no docker-compose.prod.yml or PgBouncer anywhere (grep), in-process jobs on by default. I found two further multi-instance problems the first auditor missed. docker-compose.yml runs `api` with `deploy.replicas: 2`, RUN_MIGRATIONS_ON_BOOT=true and SEED_ON_BOOT=true, yet runMigrations (packages/db/src/migrate.ts:7, drizzle's migrator) takes no advisory lock and the seed guard is a check-then-act `select count(*) from projects` (seed/run.ts:63-73), so two containers starting together race both migrations and the demo seed. And every authenticated request depends on Redis with maxRetriesPerRequest 3 and no DB fallback (plugins/redis.ts:11; auth.ts:72,92), so Redis is a single point of failure for the whole API, not just a cache. Three documented scale artifacts being absent (partitions, PgBouncer compose, S3) is more than a 'minor gap' for a factor about multi-instance readiness.

**World-best would.** Ship a working S3/GCS adapter and use it by default in production, aggregate metrics per process into a shared registry (or expose per-worker endpoints) and run jobs only in the dedicated worker deployment, with the claimed PgBouncer and partitioning artifacts actually present and tested.

Evidence: `apps/api/src/plugins/auth.ts:41-49,70-106 — sessions/permissions are JWT + Redis (revocation markers, versioned user rows); apps/api/src/lib/rateLimit.ts:9-25 — sliding-window limits in Redis MULTI, multi-instance safe; apps/api/src/plugins/idempotency.ts:52-57,100-118 — Redis fast path + Postgres durable row + Redis NX lock for concurrent duplicates`, `apps/api/src/lib/metrics.ts:1-8 — request counters/histograms are per-process Maps ('aggregation across instances is the scraper's job'); with node:cluster workers sharing one port (server.ts:70-77) a /metrics scrape lands on a random worker, so series are inconsistent and never summed`, `apps/api/src/lib/storage.ts:34 — STORAGE_DRIVER=s3 throws 'S3 storage driver not bundled in this build'; only the local-disk driver exists, so documents/media need a shared volume to run on more than one node (docker-compose.yml mounts one storage volume)`, `apps/api/src/plugins/db.ts:15-25 — primary + replica handles; 207 call sites use app.dbReplica (bookings, flats, portal, public, reports); apps/api/src/worker.ts:22-26,35-54 — BullMQ schedulers upserted idempotently so N workers run each job once`, `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md §6 claims monthly partition scripts in packages/db/partitions and PgBouncer in docker-compose.prod.yml: neither path exists (ls packages/db; only docker-compose.yml is present; grep finds 'pgbouncer' only in a client.ts comment)`, `apps/api/src/lib/list.ts:134-150 — per-process 60 s pg_class estimate cache (harmless: only picks exact vs estimated counting); apps/api/src/plugins/loadShed.ts:14 — in-flight counter is per process by design`

### C5. Hot-path efficiency — 740/1000 (v1: 360)

**Strengths.** The money hot path is append-only with running balances under a per-booking lock, list pages batch their related data in a single statement, detail endpoints page their ledgers, the grid and global search are single statements, and every list filter/sort has a matching compound index — no N+1 was found in the bookings, flats, customers, grid or search paths.

**Gaps.** The bookings list does per-row schedule computation and embeds both snapshots, which is why it is the heaviest page by 3×; postPayment's ~15 serial round trips (including a SUM over all payments for the bucket cap, a correlated SUM over demands in writeSummary and row-by-row demand status updates) cap single-booking throughput around 100/s; `recomputeDemandStatuses` and `bookingRecipients` could be folded into one statement each. Lists still run a joined count query alongside the page query.

**Second auditor.** The core claims hold on re-reading: appendEvent is lock → select booking → select last row (served by ledger_entries_booking_seq_idx) → one insert → summary update (ledger.ts:91-101); loadBookingRelated is one statement with eight JSON-aggregating scalar subqueries (views.ts:179-201); detail returns summary/charges/schedule/revisions while ledger, payments and demands are separate paged routes (bookings.ts:148-226); the grid is one four-table join (blocks.ts:343-350); search is one UNION ALL with server-side cancel (search/index.ts:24-63). No N+1 found. The first auditor's statement that 'every list filter/sort has a matching compound index' is however not true for the most-used defaults: bookings sorted by -booking_date with only a company/scope filter cannot use bookings_company_project_status_date_idx (project_id and status precede the date), customers' default -created_at has no index, and leads' default -created_at is only covered by (company_id, source, created_at). writeSummary runs a correlated SUM over demands on every append (ledger.ts:84), bucket-cap and charge-cap checks SUM over payments each posting (payments.ts:24-33), and toBookingRow recomputes the schedule per row and ships both snapshots (views.ts:230-252). These are the things that will decide latency at real row counts, so 740 rather than 780.

**World-best would.** Keep paid-per-bucket and demand status as maintained columns updated in the same statement as the ledger append, return list rows without snapshots (fetch on demand), precompute the next installment at write time instead of re-running the schedule engine per row, and collapse the payment transaction to a handful of statements.

Evidence: `apps/api/src/modules/money/services/ledger.ts:91-101 — appendEvent: lock booking → read booking → read last ledger row → domain appendLedgerRow → one insert + summary update; O(1) per payment; rebuild() (217-245) is reserved for admin recompute, revisions and the nightly integrity report`, `apps/api/src/modules/money/services/views.ts:179-227 — related data for a page of bookings in ONE statement of eight JSON-aggregating scalar subqueries (was eight parallel queries; 2.98 → 1.66 ms, verified byte-identical by test/unit/views-json.test.ts); routes/bookings.ts:125-134 list = paginate (2 queries) + 1 related query`, `apps/api/src/modules/money/routes/bookings.ts:148-151,163-183,211-226 — booking detail returns summary/charges/schedule/revisions only; ledger, payments and demands are separate paged endpoints (no 500-payment payloads)`, `apps/api/src/modules/inventory/blocks.ts:343-350 — grid is one join query (flats ⋈ flat_types ⋈ bookings ⋈ customers) grouped in memory; apps/api/src/modules/search/index.ts:24-63 — one UNION ALL statement with a 150 ms budget and server-side query.cancel()`, `apps/api/src/modules/money/services/payments.ts:71-127 — postPayment issues ~15 sequential round trips in one transaction (FOR UPDATE + advisory lock, mode, bank, bucket-cap SUM (23-28), company, sequence upsert, insert, appendEvent ×4, lastPaymentAt, recomputeDemandStatuses (dues.ts:194-206: reload all demands and UPDATE row by row), audit, recipients, notifications) → measured 8–10 ms and ~100–130 payments/s per booking`, `apps/api/src/modules/money/services/views.ts:230-252 — toBookingRow runs schedulePreview and nonVoid.find per row on every list page and ships full plan_snapshot/flat_snapshot per row (65 KB per 25 rows — the one list that misses 300 ms); packages/db/drizzle/0000_initial.sql:1579-1581,1611-1613,1636-1640 — compound indexes match the list filters/sorts`

### C6. Pagination & large-list handling — 740/1000 (v1: 430)

**Strengths.** Pagination is solved once, centrally and correctly: bounded page sizes, allow-listed sorts, microsecond-exact keyset cursors verified by tests, estimated totals for big tables, and streamed CSV with a hard cap and exact counts.

**Gaps.** The CSV path loads the whole 10k-row page and its related JSON aggregates into memory before streaming, and the promised background export for larger sets does not exist. The ERP never uses the keyset mode, so deep offset pages pay O(offset). Contains-searches use ILIKE with no trigram index, which will sequential-scan once customers/bookings reach lakhs of rows, and the estimated-count branch has only been exercised by forcing the threshold on a 4-row table — never on data above 10k rows. The count query repeats the page query's joins on every request.

**Second auditor.** Verified in code and by running the tests: paginate() (list.ts:162-201) implements both offset and keyset modes with a microsecond-exact timestamp key compared against a typed parameter, MAX_PAGE_SIZE 200, allow-listed sorts, and the keyset walk tests pass on my own database (central.test.ts cases 1 and 5 green; conventions.test.ts:97-115 covers 60 rows both directions). Also verified as the first auditor said: the estimate branch is only exercised by forcing estimateAbove: -2 on a 4-row table; CSV materialises all 10,000 BookingRow objects plus their related JSON before Readable.from streams them (bookings.ts:132-133, csv.ts:58-68); JOB_NAMES has no export job (enums.ts:785-794); the ERP never uses cursor mode (no next_cursor/cursor usage in apps/erp/src); no pg_trgm index exists (the only GIN indexes are on jsonb columns, 0001_central_fixes.sql:6-9) while search is ILIKE '%term%' on name/phone/number columns. Added to that, the default sorts lack indexes (factor 5), so deep offset pages on bookings/customers will sort the full table every request. The central implementation is genuinely good; the large-list story beyond 10k rows is unproven and partly unindexed.

**World-best would.** Cursor-paginate every ERP list by default, stream CSV rows from a server-side cursor and hand > 10k exports to the worker with a download link, add pg_trgm GIN indexes on searched text columns, and prove the estimate path against a 100k-row fixture in CI.

Evidence: `apps/api/src/lib/list.ts:162-201 — one central paginate(): offset mode (page/page_size) or keyset mode (cursor+limit, cursor = base64url(sortValue,id), limit+1 lookahead); packages/contracts/src/common.ts:183-185,192-204 — MAX_PAGE_SIZE 200, default 25, allow-listed sort`, `apps/api/src/lib/list.ts:45-65,178-199 — timestamp sorts page on a microsecond-exact to_char(... 'US') key compared against a typed parameter so the index is used; apps/api/test/integration/conventions.test.ts:97-115 — 60-row fixture visited exactly once in both directions on a tied key; central.test.ts:28-50 passes (re-run on kkt_audit_C)`, `apps/api/src/lib/list.ts:43,121-150,204-215 — exact count(*) below 10k live rows (pg_class.reltuples cached 60 s per process), planner estimate (EXPLAIN (FORMAT JSON)) above it unless exact=true; central.test.ts:53-63 exercises it only via estimateAbove: -2 on a 4-row table`, `apps/api/src/lib/list.ts:249-255 + lib/csv.ts:58-68 — format=csv = page 1 × 10,000 rows, exact count, streamed through an async generator with RFC 4180 quoting; routes/bookings.ts:132-133 — but toBookingRows first materialises all 10k rows plus one json_agg of every demand/charge for those ids before streaming`, `packages/contracts/src/enums.ts:785-794 — JOB_NAMES has no export job, so the 'larger exports belong to a worker job' comment (list.ts:248) has no implementation; apps/erp/src/lib/queries.ts:70-75 — the ERP uses offset paging (page/pageSize) everywhere, never cursors`, `apps/api/src/lib/list.ts:72-79 — search is ILIKE '%term%' across columns (customers: full_name, phone; bookings: booking_number, customer name, flat number) with btree indexes only — no trigram/GIN index exists in 0000_initial.sql or 0001_central_fixes.sql`

### C7. Connection pooling & DB load — 700/1000 (v1: 430)

**Strengths.** Pooling is explicit and sized per worker, every statement is prepared, replica reads are plumbed, rate limiting and idempotency cost Redis round trips rather than database writes, the production Postgres config is tuned with pg_stat_statements and slow-query logging, and the two pool-contention multipliers found under load (query fan-out, leaked searches) were fixed.

**Gaps.** There is no statement_timeout, lock_timeout or idle-in-transaction timeout anywhere, so a slow report or a stuck advisory lock can pin pool slots indefinitely, and there is no pool acquire timeout, so overload turns into unbounded queueing (16 s p99) unless the optional in-flight cap is enabled. PgBouncer is documented but not deployed, so `workers × replicas × pool` must be hand-tuned against max_connections. The read-replica pool is defined but no compose/deploy artifact provisions a replica.

**Second auditor.** Confirmed: postgres.js pool `max` 20 per worker, idle_timeout 30 (client.ts:92-100), replica pool half size (db.ts:16-17), every Drizzle query a named prepared statement (client.ts:45-74), rate limiting one Redis MULTI (rateLimit.ts:9-25), idempotency one Postgres row only on 2xx (idempotency.ts:107-110), tuned postgresql.conf with pg_stat_statements and 300 ms slow-query logging. Also confirmed with a repo-wide grep: no statement_timeout, lock_timeout, idle_in_transaction_session_timeout, connect_timeout or pool acquire timeout anywhere in apps/api/src, packages/db/src, infra or docker-compose.yml; the 150 ms search budget is the only bounded query. The pool arithmetic is already tight at the default deployment: compose runs 2 api replicas, each with up to 20 primary connections per HTTP worker, plus the worker container (4) and any replica pool, against max_connections 200; `API_WORKERS=auto` on 4 vCPU as DEPLOY.md instructs gives 2×4×20 = 160 before scaling, and `--scale api=3` exceeds the limit with no PgBouncer in front. One extra point: prepared statements are enabled by default, which is the right choice without PgBouncer but silently incompatible with the transaction-mode PgBouncer the docs recommend unless DATABASE_PREPARE=false is remembered. A few points lower than the first auditor because unbounded pool queueing is the direct cause of the 16 s p99 and nothing in the default configuration limits it.

**World-best would.** Set statement/lock/idle-in-transaction timeouts per role, bound pool acquisition with fast failure, front Postgres with PgBouncer by default (and ship the prod compose it claims), and expose pool saturation as a metric that drives shedding.

Evidence: `packages/db/src/client.ts:92-108 — postgres.js pool (max from DATABASE_POOL_MAX=20 per worker, idle_timeout 30 s); client.ts:40-74 wraps unsafe/begin/savepoint so every Drizzle query is a named prepared statement (planning 1.3 ms → cached; page query 3.07 → 1.23 ms), DATABASE_PREPARE=false for PgBouncer < 1.21; apps/api/src/plugins/db.ts:16-17 replica pool = half the primary`, `apps/api/src/lib/rateLimit.ts:9-25 — rate limiting is one Redis MULTI (INCR/EXPIRE/GET) per limited request, no database write; apps/api/src/plugins/idempotency.ts:107-110 — one durable idempotency row only on 2xx, with Redis as the fast path`, `infra/postgres/postgresql.conf — max_connections 200, shared_buffers 2GB, work_mem 16MB, random_page_cost 1.1, pg_stat_statements, log_min_duration_statement 300 (mounted by docker-compose.yml)`, `grep for statement_timeout/lock_timeout/idle_in_transaction_session_timeout/connect_timeout across apps/api/src, packages/db/src and infra: no hits — the 150 ms search budget (search/index.ts:52-63) is the only bounded query; postgres.js has no acquire timeout, so docs/PERFORMANCE.md 1,000-conn 'before' rows show p99 16 s and 30 s client timeouts from pool queueing`, `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md §6 and docs/DEPLOY.md describe 'PgBouncer (transaction mode) in docker-compose.prod.yml'; that file does not exist (only docker-compose.yml) and no PgBouncer service is defined anywhere`, `docs/PERFORMANCE.md 'What profiling found': list pages reduced from 8 pool connections per request to 1; search no longer leaks ~200k queued statements`

### C8. Readiness for 5 lakh — 590/1000 (v1: 210)

**Strengths.** The read side is designed the right way for a builder's traffic shape: stateless JWT workers, Redis-cached public API with CDN headers that already serves 20k rps on 4 cores, portal reads on a replica handle, durable queues for background work, and an explicit load-shedding valve; the docs do not pretend 5 lakh concurrent staff is a real workload.

**Gaps.** What would actually break first is not analysed: the website is fully dynamic SSR with uncacheable HTML and no-store detail fetches, so 5 lakh readers hit Next.js render-per-request; images are streamed through the API from local disk with no object storage or CDN; a single non-persistent LRU Redis sits on every authenticated request without a fallback; replicas, PgBouncer and partitioning exist only as doc claims or env knobs; nothing is sized (Redis ops/s, pool math, bandwidth per node). No capacity model or failure-order list exists beyond one paragraph.

**Second auditor.** The first auditor's gaps are all verified (force-dynamic website, images streamed through the API from local disk, S3 stub, single non-persistent LRU Redis as a hard dependency, no partitions/PgBouncer/autoscaling artifacts). The reason I go lower is a concrete bug that makes the marketing site fail far below 5 lakh: the auth plugin rate-limits every public route per client IP in one shared bucket `pub:all:<ip>` at PUBLIC_RATE_LIMIT_PER_MIN = 300 (auth.ts:113-116, config.ts:44), and the Next.js server calls the API over API_INTERNAL_URL with only an `accept` header (apps/web/lib/api.ts:151-158) and never forwards the visitor's address (grep x-forwarded-for in apps/web: none). All SSR traffic therefore arrives from one socket IP, and with detail pages fetched no-store twice per render (docs/qa/web.md:67) the website's own fetches start receiving 429 at roughly 150 project-page views per minute, before any cache, CDN or replica matters. Add Indian mobile CGNAT, where thousands of real visitors share one public IP, and the same limiter will 429 the portal's and website's genuine users. The honest framing in ARCHITECTURE §6 and the strong cached-read numbers (20k rps) are real, but 'what breaks first' is not the API's CPU; it is a config default and an SSR design that no document identifies.

**World-best would.** Publish a capacity model (requests, bytes and Redis/Postgres ops per 1,000 users), make website pages ISR/CDN-cacheable with CMS-driven revalidation, serve media from object storage behind a CDN, run Redis in HA with a degrade-to-DB auth path, provision read replicas and PgBouncer in the deploy artifacts, and prove it with a multi-node load test.

Evidence: `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md §6 — honest framing: 5 lakh is a public/portal read problem solved by CDN + Redis + read replicas; docs/PERFORMANCE.md — cached public reads measured 20,443 rps at 1,000 conns on 4 vCPU with Cache-Control s-maxage=60/SWR=300 (web/shared.ts:16)`, `apps/web/app/layout.tsx:17 — the website root layout is force-dynamic (prerender-manifest lists only /icon.svg as static), and apps/web/lib/api.ts:183 fetches every detail page no-store: each visitor request is an SSR render plus an API call, and Next's dynamic responses are not CDN-cacheable — the website, not the API, breaks first`, `apps/api/src/modules/web/publicContent.ts:570-590 — image bytes are streamed through the API from storage per request; apps/api/src/lib/storage.ts:34 — S3 driver throws, so there is no object store/CDN origin for media; docker-compose.yml has no CDN, one Redis (--save "", 512 MB LRU), api replicas: 2`, `apps/api/src/plugins/auth.ts:72,92 — every authenticated staff/portal request does app.redis.mget with no fallback, so Redis is a hard dependency on the hot path; portal routes read through app.dbReplica (apps/api/src/modules/portal/*.ts), and auth is stateless JWT with Redis-only revocation`, `packages/db/partitions (claimed for audit_logs/ledger_entries/notifications at ~50 M rows) does not exist; no PgBouncer; no autoscaling manifests — DEPLOY.md's 'cloud, horizontally scaled' section is prose only`, `docs/STATUS.md 'Known open items' — acknowledges the 1,000-connection miss and the S3 adapter gap`

### C9. Frontend performance — 715/1000 (v1: 570)

**Strengths.** All three fronts are built with modern toolchains: the ERP code-splits every route behind long-lived vendor chunks with immutable caching and virtualised tables, the website ships ~100–130 KB gz of JS per page with AVIF/WebP responsive images and self-hosted fonts and settles well under 1 s in QA, and the portal opens instantly from a persisted query cache.

**Gaps.** ERP first paint depends on a render-blocking third-party Google Fonts stylesheet and a 156 KB gz grab-bag vendor chunk; the 1.5 s ERP page target has no measurement at all. The portal web build is a single ~700 KB gz JS payload with no splitting — heavy for a phone on 4G. Website images are optimised by the Next server fetching from the API origin per variant with no CDN in front, and the site itself is dynamic SSR (layout.tsx:17) so HTML is never edge-cached. No bundle-size budget or Lighthouse/Web Vitals check exists in CI.

**Second auditor.** Re-checked the artefacts: apps/erp/dist has vendor 523 KB, charts 352 KB (lazy), react 264 KB, query 119 KB, index 82 KB and 87 KB CSS, index.html modulepreloads react/query/vendor and loads three Google Fonts families render-blocking (dist/index.html:12-17); vite.config.ts:8-18 defines the four vendor buckets; routes.tsx has 52 lazy() imports (the first auditor said 66 — the count is lower but the point stands); nginx.conf serves /assets immutable; data-table.tsx uses @tanstack/react-virtual. apps/portal/dist web build is a 1.12 MB entry plus a 2.06 MB __common chunk with no route splitting. apps/web uses next/image AVIF/WebP with minimumCacheTTL 3600 (next.config.ts:64-69) but the root layout is force-dynamic. QA timings exist only for the website (docs/qa/web.md:13-35, all under 750 ms at single-user load). The one correction is the lazy-route count; otherwise the first auditor's reading is accurate, and the assessment (modern toolchain, no budgets, no Lighthouse, blocking fonts, monolithic portal bundle) is fair.

**World-best would.** Self-host fonts, enforce bundle budgets and Lighthouse/Web Vitals thresholds in CI for all three apps, split the portal web bundle per route, prefetch route chunks on hover, and serve website HTML and images from a CDN with ISR.

Evidence: `apps/erp/vite.config.ts:8-18,29-37 — Vite build, es2022 target, manualChunks (react/query/charts/vendor); apps/erp/src/routes.tsx:9-82 — 66 lazy() route imports; apps/erp/dist/index.html modulepreloads react+query+vendor only (charts 352 KB raw / 97 KB gz loads on demand)`, `apps/erp/dist/assets sizes: vendor 523 KB raw / 156 KB gz, react 264 / 84, query 119 / 33, index 82 / 27, index.css 87 / 15 → ≈ 300 KB gz initial; apps/erp/nginx.conf:5-6 — gzip on, /assets immutable 1 year, index no-cache; apps/erp/dist/index.html:12-17 — Inter/JetBrains/Manrope loaded render-blocking from fonts.googleapis.com`, `apps/erp/src/app.tsx:20-27 — staleTime 15 s, gcTime 5 min, keepPreviousData in list hooks; apps/erp/src/components/ui/data-table.tsx uses @tanstack/react-virtual; NotificationBell polls every 30 s (foreground only)`, `apps/web/next.config.ts:64-69 — next/image AVIF/WebP, deviceSizes down to 390, minimumCacheTTL 3600, remotePatterns limited to /public/media; apps/web/.next build-manifest: rootMainFiles 103 KB gz, home 125 KB gz, project detail 133 KB gz; docs/qa/web.md:13-35 — every page settles < 750 ms at 1440 and 390 px`, `apps/portal/dist/_expo/static/js/web — entry 1.12 MB + __common 2.06 MB raw (296 + 413 KB gz) with no route-level splitting; apps/portal/src/api/queries.ts:28-47 — persisted TanStack cache (AsyncStorage persister, maxAge) so screens open from cache and refresh in background`, `docs/qa/erp.md — 543 page audits for console errors/overflow/targets but zero load-time numbers; no Lighthouse or Web Vitals figures for any surface`

### C10. Load-testing rigor & performance observability — 690/1000 (v1: 500)

**Strengths.** The load test is a real, repeatable, committed tool whose output is reported with unusual honesty — raw percentiles, failure counts, shed-vs-admitted splits, profiling evidence for each fix and an explicit list of what the harness cannot show — and the API exposes request ids, server timing, Prometheus text metrics and slow-query logging.

**Gaps.** Rigor stops at the harness: a toy dataset, one user token, co-located generator and database, no think time, no mixed workload, no soak or ramp, no CI gate, and a documented k6 setup that does not exist. Observability is thin for production: per-process counters that a cluster scrape cannot aggregate, a /metrics endpoint locked behind a short-lived staff JWT, only five latency buckets, no pool/Redis/queue/event-loop-lag gauges, no tracing, no dashboards or alert rules.

**Second auditor.** The harness is as described: autocannon per scenario with 30 s duration, 30 s timeout, p95 computed from every sampled response, admitted-vs-shed split, settle() between scenarios, and a fresh Idempotency-Key per write (run.ts:65-102, 150-163, 201-209); the doc records before/after tables, profiling findings and an explicit honest assessment. All limits noted by the first auditor are verified: one admin token for all scenarios (run.ts:166-168), seed-sized data, same box, no think time, no CI step (.github/workflows/ci.yml has typecheck/tests/builds only), 'k6' mentioned in ARCHITECTURE.md and the old audit but no k6 script exists (only autocannon.d.ts and run.ts in apps/api/loadtest). Observability: metrics.ts exposes per-process request counters and a 5-bucket histogram plus heap bytes; /metrics is guarded by an admin.tools staff JWT (system/index.ts:49-58), so no scraper can poll it without a login flow; no pool, Redis, queue-depth or event-loop-lag gauges; no tracing. One rigor gap the first auditor did not call out: because the harness uses one token from one IP, it is structurally unable to detect the public rate-limit ceiling that the real website hits (finding 1), and the public scenarios ran only after PUBLIC_RATE_LIMIT_PER_MIN was raised (docs/PERFORMANCE.md 'How the numbers are produced'). Honest and repeatable, but not representative, and the production observability is thin.

**World-best would.** Run a realistic scenario mix (many users, scoped roles, think time, writes across bookings) against a production-sized dataset on separate nodes as a CI-gated regression job, export aggregated RED/USE metrics (pool, Redis, queue depth, loop lag) on an unauthenticated internal metrics port with OpenTelemetry traces, and ship Grafana dashboards and SLO alerts.

Evidence: `apps/api/loadtest/run.ts:65-102 — autocannon runner computes p95 from every sampled response, splits admitted vs shed latencies, counts non-2xx/errors/timeouts; run.ts:150-163 settle() between scenarios; run.ts:201-209 write scenario with a fresh Idempotency-Key per request against the booking with most cash headroom; results appended to docs/PERFORMANCE.md (pnpm loadtest)`, `docs/PERFORMANCE.md 'How the numbers are produced', 'What profiling found' (--cpu-prof, EXPLAIN ANALYZE, per-fix before/after ms) and 'Honest assessment' — states the harness limits (same box, zero think time, one generator = one IP/user, toy seed) and which targets are missed`, `docs/SEED.md:43-45,56,59 — the load dataset is 2 projects, 130 flats, 12 customers, 10 bookings, 316 payments; one admin token for all scenarios (run.ts:166-168) so the 30 s permission cache and project-scope filters are never exercised under load`, `apps/api/src/lib/metrics.ts:1-20,27-46 — in-process Prometheus counters + 5-bucket histogram per route/method; apps/api/src/modules/system/index.ts:49-58 — /metrics requires a staff bearer token with admin.tools (15-min JWT), so an unattended scraper cannot read it; no pool/Redis/queue/event-loop gauges; per worker process behind the shared cluster port`, `apps/api/src/plugins/requestContext.ts:7-13 — X-Request-Id and X-Response-Time on every response; apps/api/src/lib/logger.ts — structured pino with redaction; infra/postgres/postgresql.conf — pg_stat_statements + log_min_duration_statement 300; apps/api/test/integration/loadshed.test.ts:11-30 — shedding behaviour tested (passes on kkt_audit_C)`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml — no load-test or performance-regression step; docs/ARCHITECTURE.md §1 and README cite 'k6/autocannon' but no k6 script exists in the repo (grep)`

## Group D — Security & Privacy — **7690 / 10,000** (previous implementation: 6360)

| # | Factor | Previous (v1) | First score | Final score |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Authentication: JWT handling, refresh rotation & reuse detection, MFA, lockout, password policy/history | 660 | 850 | **790** |
| 2 | Authorization: permission guard coverage on every endpoint | 670 | 800 | **720** |
| 3 | Field-level encryption, masking, audited reveal | 720 | 800 | **770** |
| 4 | Input validation & injection safety | 680 | 830 | **820** |
| 5 | Rate limiting & abuse protection | 610 | 780 | **760** |
| 6 | Secrets & key management | 520 | 680 | **660** |
| 7 | Audit log completeness & immutability | 630 | 820 | **790** |
| 8 | Public API allow-listing / leakage | 690 | 880 | **860** |
| 9 | File upload safety | 570 | 840 | **820** |
| 10 | Security headers, CORS | 610 | 720 | **700** |

### D1. Authentication: JWT handling, refresh rotation & reuse detection, MFA, lockout, password policy/history — 790/1000 (v1: 660)

**Strengths.** Textbook token design: short EdDSA access tokens, hashed rotating refresh families with reuse detection and an atomic rotation claim, immediate revocation via Redis session markers, argon2id, TOTP MFA with an admin-force setting, lockout (5/15 min, 423), history-aware password changes that revoke other sessions, login_events and audit rows for every auth event. 20/20 security integration tests and 16 auth tests re-ran green on a private database.

**Gaps.** Staff login leaks account existence through `details.attempts_left` (present only for real accounts) and through 423 LOCKED. The JWT and signed-payload keys are derived from one JWT_SECRET with a fixed `kid: k1`, so there is no overlap window for key rotation; rotating the secret logs everyone out at once. The forced-MFA check keys on role *names* ('Super Admin'/'Project Admin'), so a custom role with identical permissions escapes it, and the mfa_token carries the client IP but `/auth/login/mfa` never checks it. Default demo credentials are created on first production boot unless the operator remembers SEED_ON_BOOT=false, and the ERP/portal-web keep a 7-day refresh token in localStorage. No WebAuthn/passkeys, no new-device notification, no per-account login throttle beyond the lockout.

**Second auditor.** The core token design is as good as claimed: I re-read lib/jwt.ts (EdDSA, iss/aud/typ/sub/cid/sid checks), auth/service.ts (hashed rotating families, atomic claim at :157-162, reuse → family revoked + audited) and re-ran security.test.ts (20/20 green on a private database). But two of the first auditor's gaps are not theoretical: a 10-line PoC against the test app showed the unknown-e-mail 401 has no `details` while the known-account 401 carries `{attempts_left:4}` (staff.ts:78 vs :90; the 'indistinguishable' test at security.test.ts:101-107 compares only status/code/message, so it is green while the oracle exists), and five wrong passwords from five different source IPs locked the seeded Sales user so the real owner got 423 from a sixth IP — lockout is keyed only on the account, so anyone who knows a staff e-mail can lock it out every 15 minutes. I also found an unlisted gap: issueTokens (service.ts:51) stamps a fresh 7-day expiry on every rotation, so a session never has an absolute lifetime — a refresh token that keeps rotating lives forever. The mfa_token is not single-use within its 5-minute window and its `ip` is never checked (staff.ts:99,110). Production seeding of Admin@12345 is real: config.ts:28 defaults SEED_ON_BOOT to true, docker-compose.yml:22/35 runs NODE_ENV=production with `SEED_ON_BOOT: ${SEED_ON_BOOT:-true}`, and ensureBootstrap has no production guard (bootstrap.ts:27-75; only `reseed` is guarded, run.ts:131). Password policy is 'upper/lower/digit ≥10' with no breached-/common-password check (password.ts:17-25). Strong engineering, but a confirmed enumeration oracle, a trivial remote lockout DoS and default admin credentials in the production compose file are not 'minor gaps'.

**World-best would.** Return byte-identical 401 bodies for unknown and wrong-password cases, key MFA enforcement on permissions, bind MFA/refresh tokens to device context, publish a JWKS with overlapping key ids for zero-downtime rotation, refuse to seed demo users in production, and offer passkeys/WebAuthn with new-device alerts.

Evidence: `apps/api/src/lib/jwt.ts:31-55 — EdDSA (Ed25519) access tokens with iss/aud/typ/sub/cid/sid checks, 15-min expiry, WRONG_AUDIENCE reported distinctly`, `apps/api/src/modules/auth/service.ts:125-169 — refresh rotation with SHA-256-hashed tokens, atomic claim (UPDATE … WHERE replaced_by_id IS NULL), revoked/replaced token ⇒ whole family revoked + audited (TOKEN_REUSED)`, `apps/api/src/plugins/auth.ts:70-74,131-149 — per-request Redis check of revoked sid, 30 s user cache keyed by perm:version, forced password change, force_mfa_for_admins, permission check`, `apps/api/src/modules/auth/staff.ts:40-45,86-103,208-256 — argon2 burn-verify for unknown e-mails, TOTP enrol/verify/disable (otplib window ±1), mfa_token 5-min signed payload; apps/api/src/lib/password.ts:64-92 — argon2id m=64 MiB t=3, ≥10 chars policy, last-5 history`, `apps/api/src/modules/auth/staff.ts:78 vs :90 — known account returns details.attempts_left, unknown account returns no details (enumeration oracle the test at test/integration/security.test.ts:101-107 does not cover, it compares only status/code/message)`, `apps/api/src/seed/bootstrap.ts:7-13,66-73 + apps/api/src/server.ts:156-158,224-225 + apps/api/src/config.ts:120 — SEED_ON_BOOT defaults true and ensureBootstrap has no production guard, so a fresh production boot creates admin@sgb.in / Admin@12345 (and five more) with must_change_password=false`

### D2. Authorization: permission guard coverage on every endpoint — 720/1000 (v1: 670)

**Strengths.** Guard coverage is enforced structurally (route factory + registry test + /system/routes), out-of-scope data is 404 everywhere including filters, search and documents, portal ownership is a single SQL condition reused by every portal query, and adversarial suites (portal-adversarial, documents, crm-adversarial, acceptance scenario 10) probe cross-customer, cross-company and cross-audience access.

**Gaps.** The global search endpoint is a concrete permission bypass: any staff member with project.read (every system role, including the read-only Employee role) can enumerate customer names and phone numbers and booking numbers. Role/user management has no 'cannot grant more than you hold' or 'cannot change your own role' guard, so users.manage is effectively Super Admin. Login events with a null company_id are visible to every tenant's audit.view (single-tenant today). The per-entity permission matrix is checked only by hand-written tests in some modules (crm-adversarial:553) rather than a generated matrix test across all 396 staff routes.

**Second auditor.** Structural guard coverage is real: routes.ts:88-89 throws at registration unless exactly one of permission/audience/public is set, rbac.test.ts:41-56 fails on any staff route without a permission, and there are no routes registered outside the factory (grep for app.get/post in src/modules returns nothing). Audience separation (auth.ts:118-126) and the single ownership condition for the portal (portal/common.ts:37-39) hold up. However both bypasses the first auditor flagged reproduce as real privilege failures, which I verified end-to-end: (1) a custom role holding only dashboard.view + users.manage logged in, PATCHed its own user with role_id = Super Admin's id and got 200 'Super Admin'; /auth/me then reported 45 permissions — users/index.ts PATCH /users/:id has no self-change or permission-superset guard, so users.manage is a one-request path to full admin; (2) the seeded Employee role (COMMON_STAFF only, permissions.ts:217,301-304) got 403 on GET /customers but 200 on GET /search?q=Leak and ?q=98765, returning the customer's full name, customer code and full phone number (search/index.ts:40-42 has no customer.read gate and no scope on customers). The force-MFA gate keys on role names (auth.ts:29,141), so the escalated custom role above also dodges forced MFA. No row-level security in Postgres (grep returns nothing). 'Works but notable gaps' is the right band when a non-admin can self-promote.

**World-best would.** Filter search branches by the caller's permissions, add self-escalation and superset guards in users/roles, generate a permission×route×role matrix test from the registry, and consider row-level security in Postgres as a second line behind the application scope filters.
